People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. I don’t really stop to think about whether someone else is still talking or where the conversation is going. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I feel wound up a lot, like I’m on alert, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on stuff sometimes, like anyone, but I don’t have the same thought looping over and over in my head or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. I don’t really stop to think about whether someone else is still talking or where the conversation is going. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel like you need to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have routines like anybody does, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or something bad will happen. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m always the one talking the most, but I probably do jump in when I’ve got something to say. Socially, I haven’t really paid attention to whether I talk more than other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble or had problems at work or with friends because of acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has caused some problems. Mostly at work. When the crew is talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already have the answer in my head, and then I realize I talked over somebody or messed up the flow of the discussion. It doesn’t usually come from a bad place, but it can rub people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or remorseful after acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Usually right after, I’ll realize I cut someone off while they were talking through a plan, and I feel bad about it. It’s like the answer is already in my head and I jump in, but then I can tell I stepped on them or threw off the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do have trouble letting people finish sometimes. At work especially, if the other firefighters are talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll cut in. Then I realize I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking so much that others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I talk nonstop or anything. It’s more that I jump in at the wrong time. I don’t usually notice people can’t get a word in, but I do notice afterward if I interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others can't get a word in?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of just talking nonstop. I don’t usually notice people being unable to get a word in. It’s more that I interrupt or jump in too early, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others have trouble getting a word in edgewise?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I talk so much people can’t get in. I mean, I can talk, but I don’t usually see it as me taking over the whole conversation. It’s more if anything I jump in too soon, like before they’re done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. At work especially, if the other firefighters are talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll cut in. Then I realize I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking so much that others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I talk nonstop or anything. It’s more that I jump in at the wrong time. I don’t usually notice people can’t get a word in, but I do notice afterward if I interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others can't get a word in?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of just talking nonstop. I don’t usually notice people being unable to get a word in. It’s more that I interrupt or jump in too early, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others have trouble getting a word in edgewise?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I talk so much people can’t get in. I mean, I can talk, but I don’t usually see it as me taking over the whole conversation. It’s more if anything I jump in too soon, like before they’re done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity at work is affecting your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does sometimes. At work, when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already think I know the answer. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. It’s not great, and I can tell it probably rubs people the wrong way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m here because I feel like I’m always running hot—like I can’t shut the motor off. And lately it’s been starting to get me in trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I needed to talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. I feel wound up a lot, like I’m on alert, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on stuff sometimes, like anyone, but I don’t have the same thought looping over and over in my head or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. I don’t really stop to think about whether someone else is still talking or where the conversation is going. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel like you need to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. And lately it’s been starting to get me in trouble at the firehouse, so… I figured I needed to talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. I feel wound up a lot, like I’m on alert, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on stuff sometimes, like anyone, but I don’t have the same thought looping over and over in my head or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel like you need to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have routines like anybody does, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or something bad will happen. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m always the one talking the most, but I probably do jump in when I’ve got something to say. Socially, I haven’t really paid attention to whether I talk more than other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble or had problems at work or with friends because of acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has caused some problems. Mostly at work. When the crew is talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already have the answer in my head, and then I realize I talked over somebody or messed up the flow of the discussion. It doesn’t usually come from a bad place, but it can rub people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or remorseful after acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Usually right after, I’ll realize I cut someone off while they were talking through a plan, and I feel bad about it. It’s like the answer is already in my head and I jump in, but then I can tell I stepped on them or threw off the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do have trouble letting people finish sometimes. At work especially, if the other firefighters are talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll cut in. Then I realize I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking so much that others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I talk nonstop or anything. It’s more that I jump in at the wrong time. I don’t usually notice people can’t get a word in, but I do notice afterward if I interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others can't get a word in?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of just talking nonstop. I don’t usually notice people being unable to get a word in. It’s more that I interrupt or jump in too early, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others have trouble getting a word in edgewise?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I talk so much people can’t get in. I mean, I can talk, but I don’t usually see it as me taking over the whole conversation. It’s more if anything I jump in too soon, like before they’re done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity at work is affecting your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does sometimes. At work, when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already think I know the answer. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. It’s not great, and I can tell it probably rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsivity is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s causing real problems now. At the firehouse it’s started costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, and that was kind of a wake-up call. Now I feel like my chance of moving into a leadership spot is slipping, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to track the details consistently.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're always 'running hot' and acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem for about a year and five months. It really picked up when the longer rotating shifts started, and then they added more documentation and the drone duties on top of it. Since then I’ve felt like I’m running hot a lot more and acting before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or fearful in situations where there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get worked up or on edge sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m scared or nervous when there’s no danger. That’s not something I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're not doing something active?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I’m not doing something active, I get really restless. Like in command meetings, sitting still feels like my body is revving too high. I’ll be sitting there wanting to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—just do anything besides stay planted in the chair.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I feel wound up a lot, like I’m on alert, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on stuff sometimes, like anyone, but I don’t have the same thought looping over and over in my head or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. I don’t really stop to think about whether someone else is still talking or where the conversation is going. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel like you need to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have routines like anybody does, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or something bad will happen. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m always the one talking the most, but I probably do jump in when I’ve got something to say. Socially, I haven’t really paid attention to whether I talk more than other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble or had problems at work or with friends because of acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has caused some problems. Mostly at work. When the crew is talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already have the answer in my head, and then I realize I talked over somebody or messed up the flow of the discussion. It doesn’t usually come from a bad place, but it can rub people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or remorseful after acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Usually right after, I’ll realize I cut someone off while they were talking through a plan, and I feel bad about it. It’s like the answer is already in my head and I jump in, but then I can tell I stepped on them or threw off the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do have trouble letting people finish sometimes. At work especially, if the other firefighters are talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll cut in. Then I realize I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking so much that others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I talk nonstop or anything. It’s more that I jump in at the wrong time. I don’t usually notice people can’t get a word in, but I do notice afterward if I interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others can't get a word in?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of just talking nonstop. I don’t usually notice people being unable to get a word in. It’s more that I interrupt or jump in too early, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others have trouble getting a word in edgewise?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I talk so much people can’t get in. I mean, I can talk, but I don’t usually see it as me taking over the whole conversation. It’s more if anything I jump in too soon, like before they’re done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity at work is affecting your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does sometimes. At work, when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already think I know the answer. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. It’s not great, and I can tell it probably rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsivity is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s causing real problems now. At the firehouse it’s started costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, and that was kind of a wake-up call. Now I feel like my chance of moving into a leadership spot is slipping, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to track the details consistently.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're always 'running hot' and acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem for about a year and five months. It really picked up when the longer rotating shifts started, and then they added more documentation and the drone duties on top of it. Since then I’ve felt like I’m running hot a lot more and acting before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or fearful in situations where there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get worked up or on edge sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m scared or nervous when there’s no danger. That’s not something I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're not doing something active?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I’m not doing something active, I get really restless. Like in command meetings, sitting still feels like my body is revving too high. I’ll be sitting there wanting to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—just do anything besides stay planted in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or play activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah. In briefings or during drone training, I can focus for the first few minutes, but then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at the map or the screen and I missed half of what command was saying. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when waiting for something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn makes me pretty edgy—equipment checks, medical follow-ups, even just standing in line at the store. I’ll start pacing if I can, checking the time over and over, or looking for a way to get it moving faster. Sometimes I catch myself wanting to jump ahead, even when I know I shouldn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I feel wound up a lot, like I’m on alert, but I’m not sure if that’s anxiety or just how I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I get stuck on stuff sometimes, like anyone, but I don’t have the same thought looping over and over in my head or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. A lot of times at the firehouse, if we’re talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll just cut in. I don’t really stop to think about whether someone else is still talking or where the conversation is going. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel like you need to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I have routines like anybody does, but I don’t feel like I have to repeat something over and over or something bad will happen. Nothing like that that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’m always the one talking the most, but I probably do jump in when I’ve got something to say. Socially, I haven’t really paid attention to whether I talk more than other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble or had problems at work or with friends because of acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has caused some problems. Mostly at work. When the crew is talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already have the answer in my head, and then I realize I talked over somebody or messed up the flow of the discussion. It doesn’t usually come from a bad place, but it can rub people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or remorseful after acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Usually right after, I’ll realize I cut someone off while they were talking through a plan, and I feel bad about it. It’s like the answer is already in my head and I jump in, but then I can tell I stepped on them or threw off the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do have trouble letting people finish sometimes. At work especially, if the other firefighters are talking through a plan and I think I already know the answer, I’ll cut in. Then I realize I kind of stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking so much that others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I talk nonstop or anything. It’s more that I jump in at the wrong time. I don’t usually notice people can’t get a word in, but I do notice afterward if I interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others can't get a word in?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of just talking nonstop. I don’t usually notice people being unable to get a word in. It’s more that I interrupt or jump in too early, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk so much that others have trouble getting a word in edgewise?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I talk so much people can’t get in. I mean, I can talk, but I don’t usually see it as me taking over the whole conversation. It’s more if anything I jump in too soon, like before they’re done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity at work is affecting your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does sometimes. At work, when the guys are talking through a plan, I’ll cut in because I already think I know the answer. Then afterward I realize I stepped on somebody or threw off the flow of the discussion. It’s not great, and I can tell it probably rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsivity is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s causing real problems now. At the firehouse it’s started costing me trust. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, and that was kind of a wake-up call. Now I feel like my chance of moving into a leadership spot is slipping, because the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to track the details consistently.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you're always 'running hot' and acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem for about a year and five months. It really picked up when the longer rotating shifts started, and then they added more documentation and the drone duties on top of it. Since then I’ve felt like I’m running hot a lot more and acting before I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or fearful in situations where there's no apparent danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get worked up or on edge sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m scared or nervous when there’s no danger. That’s not something I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're not doing something active?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I’m not doing something active, I get really restless. Like in command meetings, sitting still feels like my body is revving too high. I’ll be sitting there wanting to stand up, walk the bay, check the rigs—just do anything besides stay planted in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or play activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah. In briefings or during drone training, I can focus for the first few minutes, but then I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at the map or the screen and I missed half of what command was saying. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn in lines or when waiting for something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Waiting my turn makes me pretty edgy—equipment checks, medical follow-ups, even just standing in line at the store. I’ll start pacing if I can, checking the time over and over, or looking for a way to get it moving faster. Sometimes I catch myself wanting to jump ahead, even when I know I shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of hyperactivity-impulsivity, such as feeling restless or fidgety, for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s been a real problem for about a year and five months now. It really started getting worse when the longer rotating shifts came in, and then the extra documentation and drone duties got added on top of that. Since then, the restlessness and fidgeting have been pretty constant."
